Module 1 · Foundations: Tones and Pinyin

The Four Tones and Neutral Tone

Mandarin has four main tones plus a neutral tone. Tones are pitch changes that change word meaning. For example, 'ma' with a high flat tone (mÄ) means 'mother', but with a rising tone (mÃ¡) means 'hemp'. In Africa, many languages like Yoruba, Igbo, and Zulu are tonal, so you already have an ear for pitch. Use that advantage. The first tone is high and flat, like holding a note. The second tone rises, like asking 'What?' in surprise. The third tone dips low then rises, like a slow nod. The fourth tone falls sharply, like a command. The neutral tone is short and light, like the 'a' in 'sofa'. Practice with the syllable 'ma': mÄ (mother), mÃ¡ (hemp), mÇ (horse), mÃ  (scold), ma (question particle). Record yourself on your phone using the Voice Memos app. Compare with a native speaker on YouTube. For African learners, tone mistakes are common because your native tones may have different contours. Spend 10 minutes daily drilling tone pairs. Use the 'Pinyin Trainer' app (free on Android) to test your tone recognition. In Kenya, Tanzania, or Nigeria, find a Chinese language partner on Tandem or HelloTalk. Ask them to correct your tones. Remember: tones are not optional; they are part of the word. A wrong tone can turn 'I want to ask' (wÃ¨n) into 'I want to kiss' (wÄn). So focus on pitch accuracy from day one..Trim()

Pinyin Initials: b, p, m, f, d, t, n, l

Pinyin initials are consonants that start a syllable. Today we cover eight: b, p, m, f, d, t, n, l. These are similar to English but with key differences. 'b' is unaspirated, like 'b' in 'bin' but softer. 'p' is aspirated, with a puff of air, like 'p' in 'pin'. Hold a tissue in front of your mouth: it should move for 'p' but not for 'b'. 'm' and 'f' are like English. 'd' is unaspirated, like 'd' in 'dog' but softer. 't' is aspirated, like 't' in 'top'. 'n' and 'l' are like English, but 'n' is often more dental. Practice with these syllables: bÄ (eight), pÄ (to lie prone), mÄ (mother), fÄ (to send), dÄ (to build), tÄ (he/she), nÄ (to take), lÄ (to pull). In Africa, many languages have similar sounds, but aspiration may not be distinctive. For example, in Swahili, 'p' and 'b' are distinct but aspiration is not phonemic. So you must train your ear. Use the 'Pinyin Chart' on Yabla or the 'Chinese Pinyin' app. Record yourself saying 'bÄ' and 'pÄ' and check if the tissue moves. Do 5 minutes of drills daily. For African learners, the 'f' sound is common, but in some languages like Hausa, 'f' is different. Focus on the puff of air for 'p', 't'. Also, 'l' and 'n' are distinct: 'n' is nasal, 'l' is oral. Practice 'nÃ ' (that) vs 'lÃ ' (spicy). Use a mirror to see your tongue position. In Nigeria, you can join a Chinese language class at the Confucius Institute in Lagos or Abuja. They provide free materials. Consistency is key: 15 minutes a day for two weeks will build muscle memory..Trim()

Pinyin Finals: a, o, e, i, u, Ã¼

Pinyin finals are vowel sounds that form the core of a syllable. Today we cover six simple finals: a, o, e, i, u, Ã¼. 'a' is like 'ah' in 'father'. 'o' is like 'or' in 'more' but rounder. 'e' is like 'uh' in 'duh' but with a smile. 'i' is like 'ee' in 'see'. 'u' is like 'oo' in 'food'. 'Ã¼' is like 'u' in French 'tu' or German 'Ã¼ber' â round your lips and say 'ee'. In Africa, many languages have these sounds, but 'Ã¼' may be new. For example, in Yoruba, there is no 'Ã¼', but you can learn by rounding your lips. Practice with these syllables: Ä (ah), Å (oh), Ä (uh), Ä« (ee), Å« (oo), Ç (Ã¼). Combine with initials: bÄ, pÅ, mÄ, dÄ«, tÅ«, nÇ (nÃ¼ with third tone). Note that after j, q, x, y, the 'Ã¼' is written as 'u' but still pronounced 'Ã¼'. For example, 'ju' is pronounced 'jÃ¼'. Use the 'Pinyin Chart' on the 'Chinese Skill' app to hear each final. Record yourself and compare. In Africa, speakers of Bantu languages may find 'e' and 'i' easy, but 'Ã¼' requires practice. Try saying 'ee' and then round your lips without moving your tongue. Do 10 repetitions. Use a mirror. Also, 'o' is often pronounced as 'aw' in some African languages, but in Mandarin it's more rounded. Listen to native speakers on 'Slow Chinese' podcast. Practice daily for 10 minutes. Use flashcards with the final on one side and a word on the other. For example, 'a' â 'mÄ' (mother). This builds association. Remember: accurate finals are crucial for being understood. A wrong final can change 'shÃ¬' (is) to 'shÃ¹' (tree). So drill until it's automatic..Trim()

Tone Mark Placement Rules

Tone marks are placed over the main vowel in a syllable. The rules are simple but must be memorized. Rule 1: If there is an 'a', the tone mark goes on 'a'. For example, 'hÇo' (good) â tone on 'a'. Rule 2: If there is no 'a' but there is 'o' or 'e', the tone mark goes on that vowel. For example, 'duÅ' (many) â tone on 'o'; 'hÄn' (very) â tone on 'e'. Rule 3: If there is no 'a', 'o', or 'e', the tone mark goes on the last vowel. For example, 'nÇ' (you) â tone on 'i'; 'hÃ¹' (house) â tone on 'u'. Rule 4: For 'iu' and 'ui', the tone mark goes on the second vowel. For example, 'liÃ¹' (six) â tone on 'u'; 'guÃ¬' (expensive) â tone on 'i'. Rule 5: For 'Ã¼', the tone mark goes on 'Ã¼' and the two dots are kept. For example, 'nÇ' (woman). In Africa, learners often misplace tone marks because they are not used to writing them. Practice by writing pinyin for common words: 'nÇ hÇo' (hello), 'xiÃ¨xie' (thanks), 'zÃ ijiÃ n' (goodbye). Use the 'Pinyin' app to check your placement. Write 20 words daily and mark the tone. For example, 'mÄma' (mother) â tone on first 'a'. 'pÃ©ngyou' (friend) â tone on 'e'. 'xÇhuan' (like) â tone on 'i'. In Nigeria, you can use a notebook to practice. Also, remember that neutral tone has no mark. For example, 'ma' in 'mÄma' is neutral. Use the 'Chinese Pinyin' website for a tone mark placement game. Spend 15 minutes daily. This rule is crucial for reading and writing. Without correct tone marks, you may confuse words. For instance, 'shÅ«' (book) vs 'shÃ¹' (tree). So master these rules early..Trim()

Common Tone Pairs and Pronunciation Drills

Tone pairs are combinations of two tones in a row. They are essential for fluent speech. The most common pairs are: 1st+1st (e.g., 'jÄ«ntiÄn' today), 1st+2nd (e.g., 'zhÅngguÃ³' China), 1st+3rd (e.g., 'kÄishÇ' to start), 1st+4th (e.g., 'gÅngzuÃ²' work), 2nd+2nd (e.g., 'xuÃ©xÃ­' to study), 2nd+3rd (e.g., 'pÃ­ngguÇ' apple), 2nd+4th (e.g., 'mÃ­ngbai' to understand), 3rd+3rd (e.g., 'nÇ hÇo' hello â note the first 3rd tone becomes 2nd), 3rd+4th (e.g., 'mÄilÃ¬' beautiful), 4th+4th (e.g., 'zÃ ijiÃ n' goodbye). In Africa, your native language may have different tone sandhi rules. For example, in Yoruba, tones change in context. But Mandarin has specific rules: when two 3rd tones meet, the first becomes 2nd. So 'nÇ hÇo' is pronounced 'nÃ­ hÇo'. Practice with the 'Tone Pair' drills on the 'Pinyin Trainer' app. Record yourself saying each pair 10 times. Use a metronome app to keep a steady rhythm. For African learners, the 3rd tone is often the hardest because it dips and rises. Practice saying 'nÇ' (you) alone, then in pairs. Also, the 4th tone is sharp and falling; don't let it rise. Use a mirror to see your jaw drop for 4th tone. In Kenya, you can join a language exchange meetup in Nairobi. Practice with a partner: one says a tone pair, the other identifies the tones. Do this for 20 minutes daily. Use the 'HelloChinese' app for tone pair exercises. Remember, tone pairs are the building blocks of sentences. Master them to sound natural. A common mistake is to pronounce each tone in isolation, but in speech, tones blend. So drill until it's automatic..Trim()

Self-Introduction with Correct Tones

Now you will learn to introduce yourself in Mandarin with correct tones. A basic self-introduction includes: greeting, name, nationality, and occupation. Start with 'NÇ hÇo' (hello). Then say 'WÇ jiÃ o...' (My name is...). For example, 'WÇ jiÃ o Ade.' (My name is Ade). Next, 'WÇ shÃ¬...' (I am...). For nationality: 'WÇ shÃ¬ NÃ ijÃ­lÃ¬yÃ  rÃ©n.' (I am Nigerian). For occupation: 'WÇ shÃ¬ xuÃ©sheng.' (I am a student). Or 'WÇ shÃ¬ lÇoshÄ«.' (I am a teacher). Practice each sentence with correct tones. 'NÇ hÇo' â 3rd tone + 3rd tone, but first becomes 2nd: 'NÃ­ hÇo'. 'WÇ' is 3rd tone. 'jiÃ o' is 4th tone. 'shÃ¬' is 4th tone. 'NÃ ijÃ­lÃ¬yÃ ' â tones: 4th, 2nd, 2nd, 4th, 4th? Actually, 'NÃ ijÃ­lÃ¬yÃ ' is NÃ i (4th), jÃ­ (2nd), lÃ¬ (4th), yÃ  (4th). Practice slowly. 'rÃ©n' is 2nd tone. 'xuÃ©sheng' â xuÃ© (2nd), sheng (neutral). 'lÇoshÄ«' â lÇo (3rd), shÄ« (1st). Record yourself on your phone. Compare with a native speaker on YouTube. For African learners, your name may have tones in your native language, but in Mandarin, you say it with Chinese tones. For example, 'Ade' might be pronounced 'ÄdÃ©' (1st, 2nd). Ask a Chinese friend to help you. In South Africa, you can join the Chinese Community Center in Johannesburg. Practice with a partner: introduce yourself, then ask 'NÇ ne?' (And you?). Do this 10 times daily. Use the 'HelloChinese' app's self-introduction lesson. Also, learn to say 'WÇ lÃ¡izÃ¬...' (I come from...). For example, 'WÇ lÃ¡izÃ¬ KÄnnÃ­yÃ .' (I come from Kenya). Tones: WÇ (3rd), lÃ¡i (2nd), zÃ¬ (4th), KÄn (3rd), nÃ­ (2nd), yÃ  (4th). To ask 'what time is it?', say 'xiÃ nzÃ i jÇ diÇn?' (Now what time?). The answer: 'xiÃ nzÃ i sÄn diÇn shÃ­wÇ fÄn' (Now 3:15). For 'what time do you...?', say 'nÇ jÇ diÇn...?' .Trim()

In Mandarin, dates are written and spoken from largest to smallest: year, month, day. The format is: number + niÃ¡n (year) + number + yuÃ¨ (month) + number + rÃ¬ (day). Measure Words for Food and Portions

Paying the Bill and Tipping Culture

When you finish eating, you need to pay. In Mandarin, ask ä¹°å (mÇidÄn) â 'the bill, please.' Or say ç»è´¦ (jiÃ©zhÃ ng) â 'settle the bill.' In Chinese restaurants in Africa, a 10% tip is polite if service was good. You can leave 10% on the table or give it directly. To say 'keep the change,' say ä¸ç¨æ¾äº (bÃº yÃ²ng zhÇo le). (yÇu fÄpiÃ o ma?) â 'Is there a receipt?' In business settings, you may need one. Practice a dialogue: You: ä¹°åã Waiter: äºååã You: æä»ç°éã Waiter: è°¢è°¢ã You: ä¸ç¨æ¾äºã Do this three times. Also learn å¤ªè´µäº (tÃ i guÃ¬ le) â 'Too expensive.' But use it politely. If the bill seems wrong, say è¿ä¸ªä¸å¯¹ (zhÃ¨ge bÃº duÃ¬) â 'This is not right.' Then ask è¯·åç®ä¸é (qÇng zÃ i suÃ n yÃ­ biÃ n) â 'Please calculate again.' Always check your bill before paying..Trim()

Module 6 · Directions and Transportation

Asking for Directions: Where is...?

In this lesson, you will learn how to ask for directions in Mandarin. The most useful phrase is "...å¨åªé?" (... zÃ i nÇlÇ?) which means "Where is ...?" For example, "æ´æé´å¨åªé?" .Trim()

When you ask, listen for key words in the answer: å·¦ (zuÇ, left), å³ (yÃ²u, right), å (qiÃ¡n, front), å (hÃ²u, back), ä¸ç´èµ° (yÄ«zhÃ­ zÇu, go straight), æ (guÇi, turn). .Trim()

Practice with real locations. In Nairobi, you might ask a Chinese shopkeeper: "è¯·é®ï¼ä¸­å½è¶å¸å¨åªé?" (QÇngwÃ¨n, gÅngdÃ¬ zÃ i nÇlÇ?) â "Where is the construction site?" Always repeat the place name clearly. Use hand gestures if needed, but try to use the Mandarin words..Trim()

.Trim()

.Trim()

.Trim()

.Trim()

.Trim()

Module 11 · Work and School

Module 12 · Culture and Social Interactions

Chinese Festivals: Spring Festival, Mid-Autumn

Chinese festivals are a gateway to understanding Chinese culture and building relationships. In Africa, you can celebrate these festivals with local Chinese communities or host your own events. Spring Festival (æ¥è, ChÅ«njiÃ©), also known as Chinese New Year, falls on the first day of the lunar calendar (between January 21 and February 20). It lasts 15 days, ending with the Lantern Festival. Key customs: cleaning the house to sweep away bad luck, decorating with red couplets (æ¥è, chÅ«nliÃ¡n) and paper cuttings, and giving red envelopes (çº¢å, hÃ³ngbÄo) with money. The reunion dinner on New Year's Eve is crucial; families gather for dishes like dumplings (é¥ºå­, jiÇozi) and fish (é±¼, yÃº), symbolizing abundance. In Africa, you can organize a potluck where each guest brings a dish; teach friends to say "Gongxi Facai" (æ­ååè´¢, GÅngxÇ FÄcÃ¡i â wishing you prosperity). Mid-Autumn Festival (ä¸­ç§è, ZhÅngqiÅ« JiÃ©) occurs on the 15th day of the 8th lunar month (September or October). It celebrates the harvest and the moon. Families eat mooncakes (æé¥¼, yuÃ¨bÇng) and gaze at the full moon. The story of Chang'e, the moon goddess, is often told. In Africa, you can buy mooncakes from Chinese supermarkets or make your own with local ingredients like lotus seed paste. Host a moon-viewing party, serve tea, and share the legend. These festivals help you connect with Chinese colleagues, friends, and business partners. For example, in Nigeria, the Chinese community in Lagos holds Spring Festival galas; attending shows respect and opens doors. Similarly, in Kenya, Mid-Autumn events at Confucius Institutes welcome locals. Use these occasions to practice Mandarin: greet people with "XÄ«nniÃ¡n kuÃ ilÃ¨" (æ°å¹´å¿«ä¹ â Happy New Year) or "ZhÅngqiÅ« jiÃ© kuÃ ilÃ¨" (ä¸­ç§èå¿«ä¹ â Happy Mid-Autumn Festival). Learn to write these phrases in characters. Understand that red symbolizes luck and joy, while white and black are for funerals. Avoid giving clocks or sharp objects as gifts. By participating, you show cultural sensitivity and build trust. This module will equip you to navigate these festivals confidently..Trim()

Gift-Giving Etiquette and Taboos

Gift-giving in Chinese culture is a delicate art that can strengthen relationships if done correctly. In Africa, where hospitality and gift exchange are common, understanding Chinese customs helps avoid misunderstandings. First, the thought counts, but the presentation matters. Always wrap gifts in red or gold paper; avoid white, black, or blue, which are associated with funerals and mourning. Do not give clocks (é, zhÅng) as they sound like "end" (ç», zhÅng) in Chinese, symbolizing death. Also avoid sharp objects like knives or scissors, which imply severing ties. Pear (æ¢¨, lÃ­) sounds like "separation" (ç¦», lÃ­), so don't give pears, especially to couples. Umbrellas (ä¼, sÇn) sound like "dispersal" (æ£, sÃ n), so they are also taboo. Good gifts include tea, fruit (but not pears), sweets, and alcohol like baijiu or wine. If giving money, put it in a red envelope (çº¢å, hÃ³ngbÄo) for festivals or special occasions. For business, give a gift from your country, such as African crafts, coffee, or artwork, but avoid anything with your company logo as it may seem cheap. When receiving a gift, accept it with both hands, and don't open it immediately unless the giver insists. In China, gifts are often refused three times before being accepted; this is polite. In Africa, you might not face this, but be prepared. If you receive a gift, reciprocate later with something of similar value. Numbers matter: 8 is lucky (sounds like "prosper"), 6 is smooth, 9 is long-lasting, but 4 sounds like death, so avoid gifts in sets of four. For weddings, give red envelopes with money (å¶æ°, ÇushÃ¹ â even numbers). For funerals, give white envelopes with money (å¥æ°, jÄ«shÃ¹ â odd numbers) and avoid red. When visiting a Chinese home, bring a small gift like fruit or tea. Don't give chrysanthemums as they are for funerals. In Africa, you can find Chinese tea shops or order online. Practice saying "XiÃ¨xie" (è°¢è°¢ â thank you) and "ZhÃ¨ shÃ¬ gÄi nÇ de" (è¿æ¯ç»ä½ ç â this is for you). Always present gifts with both hands. These details show respect and cultural awareness..Trim()

Dining Etiquette and Toasting

Dining is a central part of Chinese social and business life. In Africa, sharing a meal is also important, but Chinese dining etiquette has specific rules. When invited to a meal, arrive on time or a few minutes early. Wait to be seated; the host will indicate your seat, often facing the door or with a view. Don't start eating until the host says "QÇng" (è¯· â please) or the eldest person begins. Use chopsticks correctly: never stick them upright in rice (resembles incense for the dead), and don't tap them on the bowl. If you need to rest them, place them on the chopstick rest or across your bowl. Try a bit of everything; it's polite. The host will often serve you food with their chopsticks, which is a sign of respect. If you don't want more, leave a little food on your plate to indicate you're full. Toasting (å¹²æ¯, gÄnbÄi â dry cup) is crucial. The host will propose a toast, often with "GÄnbÄi!" meaning bottoms up. You should stand, hold your glass with both hands (or one hand on the bottom), and clink glasses with the rim lower than the elder's or host's rim to show respect. If you don't drink alcohol, you can toast with tea or juice but explain politely. In China, it's common to toast each person individually, especially the host. In Africa, you might not have this custom, but participating shows effort. Learn phrases: "WÇ jÃ¬ng nÇ" (ææ¬ä½  â I toast you), "GÄnbÄi" (å¹²æ¯ â cheers), "SuÃ­biÃ n" (éä¾¿ â help yourself). After the meal, the host may fight to pay the bill; let them, but offer to pay next time. Bring a small gift like fruit or sweets. Don't discuss business during the meal unless the host brings it up; meals are for relationship-building. If you're hosting, choose a restaurant with private rooms, order more food than needed, and ensure everyone's glass is filled. In Africa, you can find Chinese restaurants in major cities; practice ordering in Mandarin. Say "WÇ yÃ o zhÃ¨ge" (æè¦è¿ä¸ª â I want this) pointing at the menu. This builds confidence..Trim()

Making Friends and Social Invitations

Building friendships with Chinese speakers in Africa opens doors to language practice and cultural exchange. Start by joining local Chinese community groups, Confucius Institutes, or business associations. In countries like South Africa, Nigeria, Kenya, and Ethiopia, there are active Chinese chambers of commerce and cultural centers. Attend events like Spring Festival galas or Mid-Autumn celebrations. Introduce yourself simply: "NÇ hÇo, wÇ jiÃ o [Your Name]" (ä½ å¥½ï¼æå«[åå­] â Hello, my name is...). Ask "NÇ ne?" (ä½ å¢? â And you?). Exchange WeChat IDs (å¾®ä¿¡, WÄixÃ¬n) â the main social app in China. In Africa, many Chinese expats use WeChat to connect. To make friends, show genuine interest in Chinese culture. Invite someone for coffee or a meal. Use the phrase "NÇ yÇu kÃ²ng ma?" (ä½ æç©ºå? â Are you free?) and suggest a time: "MÃ­ngtiÄn xiÃ wÇ sÄn diÇn, zÄnmeyÃ ng?" (æå¤©ä¸åä¸ç¹ï¼æä¹æ ·? â Tomorrow at 3 PM, how about?). If they agree, say "TÃ i hÇo le!" (å¤ªå¥½äº! â Great!). Be punctual; Chinese culture values timeliness. When inviting to your home, clean and tidy up, offer tea and snacks. In Africa, you might serve local dishes; explain what they are. For invitations to their home, bring a small gift (see Lesson 2). Accepting invitations: "WÇ hÄn gÄoxÃ¬ng qÃ¹" (æå¾é«å´å» â I'm happy to go). Declining politely: "DuÃ¬buqÇ, wÇ yÇu shÃ¬" (å¯¹ä¸èµ·ï¼ææäº â Sorry, I have something). Don't be surprised if they refuse your first invitation; they might be polite. Persist gently. Social media: follow Chinese friends on WeChat Moments (æåå, pÃ©ngyÇu quÄn) and like their posts. Comment in simple Mandarin: "HÄn hÇo!" (å¾å¥½! â Very good!). To deepen friendship, remember important dates like their birthday or Spring Festival. Send a red envelope via WeChat for festivals. In Africa, you can also join language exchange meetups. Practice phrases like "WÇmen zuÃ² pÃ©ngyÇu ba" (æä»¬åæåå§ â Let's be friends). Over time, you'll build a network that helps with business and cultural understanding..Trim()

Discussing Chinese Customs and Traditions

To engage in meaningful conversations about Chinese customs, you need vocabulary and cultural knowledge. Start with basic terms: ä¼ ç» (chuÃ¡ntÇng â tradition), ä¹ ä¿ (xÃ­sÃº â custom), æå (wÃ©nhuÃ  â culture), èæ¥ (jiÃ©rÃ¬ â festival). When discussing, show curiosity: "ZhÃ¨ge xÃ­sÃº yÇu shÃ©nme yÃ¬si?" (è¿ä¸ªä¹ ä¿æä»ä¹ææ? â What does this custom mean?). Common topics: the importance of family (å®¶åº­, jiÄtÃ­ng), respect for elders (å°æ¬èäºº, zÅ«njÃ¬ng lÇorÃ©n), and the concept of "face" (é¢å­, miÃ nzi). Face means reputation and dignity; avoid causing someone to lose face publicly. In Africa, where community and respect are also valued, you can draw parallels. For example, both cultures value hospitality. Discuss how Chinese people celebrate weddings: red decorations, tea ceremony (è¶ç¤¼, chÃ¡ lÇ), and giving red envelopes. Funerals: white and black, odd numbers of money. Also, talk about the Chinese zodiac (çè, shÄngxiÃ o) â 12 animals, each year has one. Ask "NÇ shÇ shÃ©nme?" (ä½ å±ä»ä¹? â What is your zodiac animal?). In Africa, many know their birth year but not zodiac; share yours. The number 8 is lucky (å, fÄ â prosper), 4 is unlucky (æ­», sÇ â death). Colors: red for luck, yellow for royalty, white for mourning. Tea culture (è¶æå, chÃ¡ wÃ©nhuÃ ) is rich; learn to say "QÇng hÄ chÃ¡" (è¯·åè¶ â Please drink tea). Food symbolism: fish for abundance, dumplings for wealth, noodles for longevity. In Africa, you can compare with local traditions. When discussing, be respectful; don't criticize. Use phrases like "ZhÄn yÇuqÃ¹!" (çæè¶£! â Really interesting!) or "WÇ xuÃ© dÃ o le" (æå­¦å°äº â I learned something). If you don't understand, say "QÇng zÃ i shuÅ yÄ« biÃ n" (è¯·åè¯´ä¸é â Please say again). To practice, watch Chinese movies or documentaries with subtitles. Join online forums. This module's final project will help you present a cultural topic in Mandarin. Remember, the goal is to build bridges, not to be perfect..Trim()

Final Project: Cultural Presentation in Mandarin

Your final project is a 5-7 minute presentation in Mandarin on a Chinese cultural topic, delivered to your class or a small group. This will test your language skills and cultural understanding. Choose a topic from this module: festivals, gift-giving, dining, friendship, or customs. For example, you could present on Spring Festival traditions, comparing them to a similar African festival. Or explain Chinese dining etiquette and demonstrate a toast. The presentation must be in Mandarin, but you can use visual aids (slides, props) to support. Start with a greeting: "DÃ jiÄ hÇo" (å¤§å®¶å¥½ â Hello everyone). Introduce your topic: "JÄ«ntiÄn wÇ yÃ o jiÃ¨shÃ o..." (ä»å¤©æè¦ä»ç»... â Today I will introduce...). Then, cover 3-4 key points. For instance, if presenting on Mid-Autumn Festival: 1) Date and legend of Chang'e, 2) Mooncakes and their meaning, 3) How it's celebrated in Africa (e.g., in Kenya, Confucius Institute events). Use simple sentences. Practice pronunciation; record yourself. Aim for clarity, not speed. Use props like a mooncake or red envelope. Engage the audience: ask "NÇmen zhÄ«dÃ o ma?" (ä½ ä»¬ç¥éå? â Did you know?). End with a conclusion: "XiÃ¨xie dÃ jiÄ" (è°¢è°¢å¤§å®¶ â Thank you everyone). Prepare a Q&A in Mandarin; anticipate questions like "NÇ xÇhuÄn zhÃ¨ge jiÃ©rÃ¬ ma?" (ä½ åæ¬¢è¿ä¸ªèæ¥å? â Do you like this festival?). To prepare, write a script in pinyin and characters, then memorize key phrases. Practice with a language partner. Time yourself. On presentation day, speak slowly and clearly. If you forget a word, use simpler terms. This project builds confidence and demonstrates your ability to apply cultural knowledge. It also prepares you for real-life interactions with Chinese speakers in Africa. After the presentation, ask for feedback. This is the culmination of Module 12, and it will showcase your progress in Mandarin and cultural competence..Trim()
